)

\hskip-12.2917pt(E,D)=\Big(\begin{pmatrix}k+1\\
k\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{x}&\mathbf{y}\\
\mathbf{0}&(0,\leq)&(0,\leq)&(-\mathit{frac}(p_{1})+\mathit{frac}(p_{2}),<)\\
\mathbf{x}&(0,\leq)&(0,\leq)&(-\mathit{frac}(p_{1})+\mathit{frac}(p_{2}),<)\\
\mathbf{y}&(1-\mathit{frac}(p_{2}),<)&(1-\mathit{frac}(p_{2}),<)&(0,\leq)\end{pmatrix}\Big)

4.4.
Non-parametric guard

From
[
AD94
, Section 4.2]
we have that either every clock valuation of a clock region satisfies a guard, or none of them does.
Note that a
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM for
R_{p}
is contained into a clock region of
2.3
(see
I.1
for more details), therefore
we have that if
w\in(E,v(D))
satisfies a non-parametric guard
g
,
then for all
w^{\prime}\in(E,v(D))
we also have
w^{\prime}
satisfies
g
.

Let
v\in R_{p}
. We define
v\in\mathit{guard}_{\forall}(g,E,D)
iff for all
w\in(E,v(D))
,
w\models g
.
As any two
v,v^{\prime}\in R_{p}
satisfy the same constraints,
the following lemma is straightforward

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
(E,D)
be a
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM for
R_{p}
and
v\in R_{p}
.
Let
g
be a non-parametric guard.
If
v\in\mathit{guard}_{\forall}(g,E,D)
, then for all
v^{\prime}\in R_{p}
,
v^{\prime}\in\mathit{guard}_{\forall}(g,E,D)
.

Proof.

See
Appendix

I
.

∎

4.5.
Parametric guard

As for the previous result, using a projection on parameters
i. e.,
eliminating clocks, does not create new constraints on parameters
that are not already in a parameter region
R_{p}
.
Indeed, a parametric guard
g
only adds new constraints of the form
x\bowtie p
which gives,
when eliminating clocks in both a
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM
(E,D)
and a parametric guard,
again a comparison between elements of
\mathcal{PLT}
. Therefore, these new constraints already belong to
\mathcal{PLT}
and we can decide whether the set of clock valuations satisfying
these constraints is non-empty
i. e.,
given
v\in R_{p}
,
v(g)
is satisfied by some clock valuation
w\in(E,v(D))
.
This is a key point in the overall process of proving the decidability of our R-U2P-PTAs.

Note that there will also be additional constraints involving clocks (with other clocks, constants or parameters), but they will not be relevant as we immediately update all clocks, therefore replacing these constraints with new constraints encoding the clock updates.

Let
v\in R_{p}
. We define
v\in\mathit{p\mbox{-}guard}_{\exists}(g,E,D)
iff there is a
w\penalty\ {\in(E,v(D))}
s.t.
w\models v(g)
.
3
3

3

Remark that here is why our construction works for EF-emptiness, but cannot be used for,
e.g.
, AF-emptiness (“is there a parameter valuation such that all runs reach a goal location
\ell
”): unlike
\mathit{guard}_{\forall}(g,E,D)
, not all clock valuations in a
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM
(E,v(D))
can satisfy a parametric guard if
v\in\mathit{p\mbox{-}guard}_{\exists}(g,E,D)
.

Again, as any two
v,v^{\prime}\in R_{p}
satisfy the same constraints,
the following lemma is straightforward

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
(E,D)
be a
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM for
R_{p}
and
v\in R_{p}
.
Let
g
be a parametric guard.
If
v\in\mathit{p\mbox{-}guard}_{\exists}(g,E,D)
, then for all
v^{\prime}\in R_{p}
,
v^{\prime}\in\mathit{p\mbox{-}guard}_{\exists}(g,E,D)
.

Proof.

See
Appendix

J
.

∎

Now that we have defined useful operations on
\mathsf{p}
–PDBMs, we are going, given a parameter region
R_{p}
, to construct a finite region automaton in which for any run, there is an equivalent concrete run in the R-U2P-PTA.

5.
Parametric region automaton

Let
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}(R_{p})
, we say
(E^{\prime},D^{\prime})\in\mathsf{Succ}((E,D))\Leftrightarrow\exists\penalty\ {i\geq 0}

s.t.
(E^{\prime},v(D^{\prime}))=\mathit{TE}^{i}((E,D))
.
In other words,
(E^{\prime},D^{\prime})
is obtained after applying
\mathit{TE}((E,D))
a finite number of times.

\mathsf{Succ}((E,D))
is also called the
time successors
of
(E,D)
.

In order to finitely simulate an R-U2P-PTA, we create a parametric region automaton.

Definition 5.1

(Parametric region automaton)
.

Let
R_{p}
be a parameter region.
For an R-U2P-PTA
\mathcal{A}=(\Sigma,L,\ell_{0},\mathbb{X},\mathbb{P},\zeta)
,
given
(E_{0},D_{0})
the initial
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM where all clocks are
0
, the
parametric region automaton

\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{A})
over
R_{p}

is the tuple
(L^{\prime},\Sigma,L_{0}^{\prime},\zeta^{\prime})
where:

(1)

L^{\prime}=L\times p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}(R_{p})

(2)

L_{0}^{\prime}=(\ell_{0},(E_{0},D_{0}))

(3)

\zeta^{\prime}=\{\big((\ell,(E,D)),a,(\ell^{\prime},(E^{\prime},D^{\prime}))\big)\in L^{\prime}\times\Sigma\times L^{\prime}\mid\text{either }\exists e=\langle\ell,g,a,u_{np},\ell^{\prime}\rangle\in\zeta
,

g
is a non-parametric guard,

\exists(E^{\prime\prime},D^{\prime\prime})\in\mathsf{Succ}((E,D))
,
R_{p}\subseteq\mathit{guard}_{\forall}(g,(E^{\prime\prime},D^{\prime\prime}))

and
(E^{\prime},D^{\prime})=\mathit{update}(E^{\prime\prime},D^{\prime\prime},u_{np})
is an
\mathsf{open}
–
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM,
or
\exists e=\langle\ell,g,a,u,\ell^{\prime}\rangle\in\zeta
,
g
is a parametric guard,

\exists(E^{\prime\prime},D^{\prime\prime})\in\mathsf{Succ}((E,D))
,
R_{p}\subseteq\mathit{p\mbox{-}guard}_{\exists}(g,(E^{\prime\prime},D^{\prime\prime}))

and
(E^{\prime},D^{\prime})=\overline{\mathit{update}}(E^{\prime\prime},D^{\prime\prime},u)
is a
\mathsf{point}
–
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM.}

Let
R_{p}
be a parameter region,
\mathcal{A}
be an R-U2P-PTA and
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{A})=(L^{\prime},\Sigma,L_{0}^{\prime},\zeta^{\prime})
its parametric region automaton over
R_{p}
.
A run in
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{A})
is an untimed sequence

\sigma:(\ell_{0},(E_{0},D_{0}))e_{0}(\ell_{1},(E_{1},D_{1}))e_{1}\cdots(\ell_{i},(E_{i},D_{i}))e_{i}(\ell_{i+1},(E_{i+1},D_{i+1}))e_{i+1}\cdots

such that for all
i
we have
\big((\ell_{i},(E_{i},D_{i})),a_{i},(\ell_{i+1},(E_{i+1},D_{i+1}))\big)\in\zeta^{\prime}
,
which we also write
(\ell_{i},(E_{i},D_{i}))\overset{e_{i}}{\longrightarrow}(\ell_{i+1},(E_{i+1},D_{i+1}))
.
Note that we label our transitions with the edges of the R-U2P-PTA.

6.
Decidability of EF-emptiness and synthesis

Using our construction of the parametric region automaton
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{A})
for a given R-U2P-PTA
\mathcal{A}
, we state the next proposition.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
R_{p}
be a parameter region. Let
\mathcal{A}
be an R-U2P-PTA and
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{A})
its parametric region automaton over
R_{p}
.
There is a run
\sigma:(\ell_{0},(E_{0},D{}_{0}))\overset{e_{0}}{\longrightarrow}(\ell_{1},(E{}_{1},D{}_{1}))\overset{e_{1}}{\longrightarrow}\cdots(\ell_{f-1},(E{}_{f-1},D{}_{f-1}))\overset{e_{f-1}}{\longrightarrow}(\ell_{f},(E{}_{f},D{}_{f}))

in
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{A})
iff for all
v\in R_{p}
there is a run
\rho:(\ell_{0},w_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{0}}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\ell_{1},w_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots(\ell_{f-1},w_{f-1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{f-1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\ell_{f},w_{f})

in
v(\mathcal{A})

s.t. for all
0\leq i\leq f
,
w_{i}\in(E_{i},v(D_{i}))
.

Proof.

We prove this result by induction on the length of the run. It is quite direct as we construct runs without parametric guards.
See
Appendix

K
for details.

∎

Example 6.2
.

Consider
Fig.

4
.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be an R-U2P-PTA,
R_{p}
a parameter region and
v\in R_{p}
.
Suppose there is a run in
\mathcal{A}
, starting from the initial location
(\ell_{0},\vec{0})
reaching a goal location
(\ell_{f},w_{f})
.
Along this run, all edges are non-parametric transitions but
\hbox{\pagecolor{yellow}$e_{i}$}=\langle\ell_{i},g,a_{i},u,\ell_{i+1}\rangle
.
That is,
u
is a total parametric update,
and
g
is a possibly parametric guard.

The first part of this run, from
(\ell_{0},\vec{0})
to
(\ell_{i},w_{i})
starts from
(l_{0},(E_{0},D_{0}))
where
(E_{0},D_{0})
is the
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM of the initial clock region
\{\vec{0}\}
,
and ends in
(\ell_{i},(E_{i},D_{i}))
. The second part of this run, from
(\ell_{i+1},w_{i+1})
to
(\ell_{f},w_{f})

starts from
(l_{i+1},(E_{i+1},D_{i+1}))
where
(E_{i+1},D_{i+1})
is a
\mathsf{point}
–
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM,
and can reach
(\ell_{f},(E_{f},D_{f}))
and further ends in
(\ell_{s},(E_{s},D_{s}))
.

These runs contain only non-parametric transitions, and as there is an edge in
\mathcal{A}
from
(\ell_{i},w_{i})
to
(\ell_{i+1},w_{i+1})
,
we have to bisimulate this run in
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{A})

containing the parametric transition
e_{i}
, where
\overline{\mathit{update}}((E_{i},D_{i}),u)
gives
(E_{i+1},D_{i+1})
.

(\ell_{0},\vec{0})
(\ell_{1},w_{1})
\cdots
(\ell_{i},w_{i})
(\ell_{i+1},w_{i+1})
\cdots
(\ell_{j},w_{j})
(\ell_{f},w_{f})
e_{0}
e_{1}
e_{i-1}
e_{i}
e_{i+1}
e_{j-1}
e_{j}

(a)

run of
\mathcal{A}
with one parametric transition
e_{i}

(\ell_{0},(E_{0},D_{0}))
(\ell_{1},(E_{1},D_{1}))
\cdots
(\ell_{i},(E_{i},D_{i}))
(\ell_{i+1},(E_{i+1},D_{i+1}))
\cdots
(\ell_{j},(E_{j},D_{j}))
(\ell_{f},(E_{f},D_{f}))
e_{0}
e_{1}
e_{i-1}
e_{i}
e_{i+1}
e_{j-1}
e_{j}

(b)

run of
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{A})
with one parametric transition
e_{i}

Figure 4
.
A run in an R-U2P-PTA
\mathcal{A}
(above) and its equivalent run in
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{A})
(below)

From
6.1
, we deduce that if there is a run reaching a goal location in an instantiated R-U2P-PTA,
then for another parameter valuation in the same parameter region there is a run in the instantiated R-U2P-PTA with the same locations and transitions (but possibly different delays), reaching the same location.

Theorem 6.3
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an R-U2P-PTA.
Let
R_{p}
be a parameter region and
v\in R_{p}
.
If there is a run
\rho=(\ell_{0},w_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{0}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{i-1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\ell_{i},w_{i})
in
v(\mathcal{A})
,
then for all
v^{\prime}\in R_{p}

there is a run
\rho^{\prime}=(\ell_{0},w_{0}^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{0}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{i-1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\ell_{i},w_{i}^{\prime})
in
v^{\prime}(\mathcal{A})

with for all
0\leq j\leq i
, there is
(E_{j},D_{j})\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}(R_{p})
s.t.
w_{j}\in(E_{j},v(D_{j}))
and
w_{j}^{\prime}\in(E_{j},v^{\prime}(D_{j}))
.

Proof.

Let
v\in R_{p}
and
\rho
a run of
v(\mathcal{A})
reaching
(\ell_{i},w_{i})
.
From
6.1
, there is a run
\sigma
in
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{A})

s.t.
each clock valuation at a location
j
in
\rho
is in the
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM
(E_{j},D_{j})
at the same location in
\sigma
.
Still from
6.1
, for all
v^{\prime}\in R_{p}
there is a run
\rho^{\prime}
in
v^{\prime}(\mathcal{A})
reaching
(\ell_{i},w_{i}^{\prime})

s.t.
each clock valuation at a location
j
in
\rho^{\prime}
is in the
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM
(E_{j},D_{j})
at the same location in
\sigma
(note that possibly
v=v^{\prime}
).
Therefore, we have
for all
0\leq j\leq i
, there is
(E_{j},D_{j})\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}(R_{p})

s.t.

w_{j}\in(E_{j},v(D_{j}))
and
w_{j}^{\prime}\in(E_{j},v^{\prime}(D_{j}))

and the expected result.

∎

Note that there is a finite number of
\mathsf{p}
–PDBMs for each parameter region
R_{p}
.
Let
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}(R_{p})
and consider
\mathcal{PLT}
:
D
is an
{(H+1)}^{2}
matrix made of pairs
(d,\triangleleft)

where
d\in\mathcal{PLT}
and
{\triangleleft\in}\{\leq,<\}
.
Therefore the number of possible
D
is bounded by

{\left(2\times\left(2+3\times\binom{M}{2}+4\times M\right)\right)}^{{(H+1)}^{2}}.

Moreover the number of
E
is unbounded, but only a finite subset of all values needs to be explored,
i. e.,
those smaller than
K+1
: indeed, following classical works on timed automata
[
AD94
,
BDFP04
]
, (integer) values exceeding the largest constant used in the guards or the parameter bounds are equivalent.

To test EF-emptiness given an R-U2P-PTA
\mathcal{A}
and a goal location
\ell
, we first enumerate all parameter regions (which are a finite number),
and apply for each
R_{p}
the following process:
we pick
v\in R_{p}
(
e.g.
using a linear programming algorithm
[
Kar84
]
).
Then, we consider
v(\mathcal{A})
which is an updatable timed automaton and test the reachability of
\ell
in
v(\mathcal{A})

[
BDFP04
]
.
Then EF-emptiness is false if and only if there is
v
and a run in
v(\mathcal{A})
reaching
\ell
.

Theorem 6.4
.

The EF-emptiness problem is PSPACE-complete for bounded R-U2P-PTAs.

Proof.

Since a TA is a special case of
R-U2P-PTA
we have the PSPACE-hardness
[
AD94
]
.
Now, let
G
be a set of goal locations of
\mathcal{A}
.
We build a non-deterministic Turing machine that:

(1)

takes
\mathcal{A}
,
G
and
K
as input;

(2)

non-deterministically “guesses” a parameter region
R_{p}
;

(3)

takes
v\in R_{p}
and writes it to the tape;

(4)

overwrites on the tape each parameter
p
by
v(p)

giving the updatable TA
v(\mathcal{A})
;

(5)

solves reachability in
v(\mathcal{A})
for
G
;

(6)

accepts iff the result of the previous step is “yes”.

The machine accepts iff there is an integer valuation
v
bounded by
K

and a run in
v(\mathcal{A})
reaching a location
\ell\in G
.

The size of the input is
|\mathcal{A}|+|G|+|K|
, using
|.|
to denote the size in bits
of the different objects.
Moreover, the number of parameter regions is bounded (
M
is the number of parameters in
\mathcal{A}
)
by
\big(M!\times 2^{M}\times\prod_{p\in\mathbb{P}}(2M+2)\big)\times\big(2\times{(2+M(3\frac{M-1}{2}+4))}^{3}\big)

since they are constructed as the clock regions of
[
AD94
]
, the second part being the maximal number of constraints in a parameter region.
Picking
v
at step (3) uses a PSPACE linear programming algorithm (
e.g.

[
Kar84
]
).
Storing the valuation at step (4) uses at most
M\times|K|

additional bits, which is polynomial
w.r.t.
the size of the input.
Step (5) also needs polynomial space from
[
BDFP04
]
.
So globally this non-deterministic machine runs in polynomial space.
Finally, by Savitch’s theorem we have
\text{PSPACE}=\text{NPSPACE}

[
Sav70
]
, and the expected result.

∎

Given a goal location
\ell
and a bounded R-U2P-PTA
\mathcal{A}
, we can exactly synthesize the parameter valuations
v
s.t. there is a run in
v(\mathcal{A})
reaching
\ell
by enumerating each parameter region (of which there is a finite number) and test if
\ell
is reachable for one of its parameter valuations.
The result of the synthesis is the union of the parameter regions for which one valuation (and, from our results, all valuations in that region) indeed reaches the goal location in the instantiated TA.

Corollary 6.5
.

Given a bounded R-U2P-PTA
\mathcal{A}
and a goal location
\ell
we can effectively compute the set of parameter valuations
v
s.t. there is a run in
v(\mathcal{A})
reaching
\ell
.

Proof.

The procedure to obtain synthesis is as follows.
We assume an
R-U2P-PTA

\mathcal{A}
and a goal location
\ell
.

(1)

enumerate all parameter regions (of which there is a finite number);

(2)

for each
R_{p}
, pick a parameter valuation we pick
v\in R_{p}
(
e.g.
using a linear programming algorithm
[
Kar84
]
);

(3)

test the reachability of
\ell
in the updatable timed automaton
v(\mathcal{A})
, which is decidable
[
BDFP04
]
;

(4)

if
\ell
is reachable in
v(\mathcal{A})
, add
R_{p}
to the list of synthesized regions.

We finally return the union of all regions
R_{p}
that reach
\ell
.

The correctness immediately comes from
6.3
and
6.4
.

∎

Remark 6
.

By bounding parameter valuations in guards but not those used in updates, we still have a finite number of parameter regions.
Indeed, an integer vector
E
with components
E_{x}
greater than
\lfloor K\rfloor+1
is equivalent to an
integer vector
E^{\prime}
with
E_{x}^{\prime}=E_{x}
if
E_{x}<\lfloor K\rfloor+1
and
E_{x}^{\prime}=\lfloor K\rfloor+1

if
E_{x}\geq\lfloor K\rfloor+1
.
Moreover for all
p
, we have to replace each parameter valuation
v

used in an update by
v(p)=v^{\prime}(p)
if
v(p)\leq K

and
v^{\prime}(p)=K+1
if
v(p)>K
.

7.
Parametric updates and stopwatches

In this section, we consider clocks in R-U2P-PTAs as
stopwatches

[
CL00
]
: stopwatches can be stopped and started again on transitions. In the general case, stopwatches bring back undecidability in timed automata
[
BBR06
]
. Similarly to the “initialization” constraint of
[
HKPV98
]
we allow stopwatches to be stopped and started again only on specific transitions.
We define
\mathcal{A}
an R-U2P-PTA with stopwatches instead of clocks. We will prove by using our
\mathsf{p}
–PDBMs structure that the
\mathsf{EF}
-emptiness problem is decidable under the condition that stopwatches can be stopped at each full update function, and started again at the next full update function.
Such a condition, as in
3.1
is critical: allowing a partial (or empty) update of clocks ruins the efforts made to keep the set of
\mathsf{p}
–PDBMs stable and allows accumulation of parameters, leading to the undecidability of the
\mathsf{EF}
-emptiness problem.

Definition 1
.

A stopwatch reset update-to-parameter PTA (S-R-U2P-PTAs)

\mathcal{A}
is a tuple
\mathcal{A}=(\Sigma,L,\ell_{0},\mathbb{X},\mathbb{P},\zeta,\mathsf{stop})
, where:

(1)

\Sigma
is a finite set of actions,

(2)

L
is a finite set of locations,

(3)

\ell_{0}\in L
is the initial location,

(4)

\mathbb{X}
is a finite set of stopwatches,

(5)

\mathbb{P}
is a finite set of parameters,

(6)

\zeta
is a finite set of edges
e=\langle\ell,g,a,u,\ell^{\prime}\rangle

where

\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in L
are the source and target locations,
g
is a parametric guard,
a\in\Sigma
and

u:\mathbb{X}\rightharpoonup\mathbb{N}\cup\mathbb{P}
is a parametric update function.

(7)

\mathsf{stop}:L\to 2^{\mathbb{X}}
assigns to each location a set of stopwatches that are stopped at this location.

Moreover,
u
is a total function whenever:

(1)

g
is a parametric guard,

(2)

u(x)\in\mathbb{P}
for some
x\in\mathbb{X}
, or

(3)

\mathsf{stop}(\ell)\neq\mathsf{stop}(\ell^{\prime})
for
e=\langle\ell,g,a,u,\ell^{\prime}\rangle
.

The semantics is defined in a straightforward manner.
The update and time elapsing operators are defined in a similar manner as for R-U2P-PTA.

Theorem 2
.

The EF-emptiness problem is PSPACE-complete for bounded S-R-U2P-PTAs.

Proof 7.1
.

To prove this result, we first remove stopped stopwatches from
\mathsf{p}
–PDBMs, and show that we can still reason as in
6.4
.

Note that after a full update, stopped stopwatches satisfy constraints defined by a
p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\odot}(R_{p})
.
These constraints, defined in
3.6
, are

(
1
)

For all
i
,
(-1,<)\leq D_{0,i}\leq(0,\leq)
and
(0,\leq)\leq D_{i,0}\leq(1,<)
are valid for
R_{p}
,

(
2
)

For all
i,j,k
,
D_{i,j}\leq D_{i,k}+D_{k,j}
is valid for
R_{p}
(canonical form).

Let
x
be a stopped stopwatch, we remove the column and the row corresponding to
x
in the
\mathsf{point}
–
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM, and we put these constraints aside.

(
1
) is still satisfied for stopwatches different from
x
as nothing changed.
As both the column and the row are removed for
x
, constraints of the form
D_{x,j}\leq D_{x,k}+D_{k,j}
and
D_{i,x}\leq D_{i,k}+D_{k,x}
are removed as well. With the same argument, constraints of the form
D_{i,j}\leq D_{i,x}+D_{x,j}
are also removed. Only constraints of the form
D_{i,j}\leq D_{i,k}+D_{k,j}
where
i,j,k
are different from
x
remain, and therefore (
2
) is still satisfied.
We apply the same reasoning for all stopped stopwatches.

After this modification, we still have a
\mathsf{point}
–
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM.
Lemmas
4.3
,
4.4
and
4.10
and
4.11
are still applicable in this context.

Remains the comparison with non parametric guards
4.12
and parametric guards
4.13
.

As stated, constraints on stopped stopwatches are put aside, call these constraints
C_{\mathsf{stop}}
; as these constraints are the result of a full update it means there is one clock valuation that satisfies these constraints (it is not an interval, or a set of clock valuations). This argument is crucial in the following: we do not need to be able to bound difference of a stopped stopwatch and a running stopwatch (i. e.,
D_{x,y}
in the
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM for some stopped
x
and running
y
, or conversely) to compare separately the
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM with a guard, parametric or not.

First, we compare the
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM with a possibly parametric guard. Let
R_{p}
a parameter region and
v\in R_{p}
.
Separately we compare the guard
g
with the constraints on stopped stopwatch
C_{\mathsf{stop}}
:

•

If
g
is a non parametric guard, let
x
be a stopped stopwatch appearing in
g
.

–

If
x
has been updated to a constant, we can test whether
g
is satisfied by testing the conjunction of the constraints
v(C_{\mathsf{stop}})
projected on
x
and
g
projected on
x
.

–

If
x
has been updated to a parameter, we can test the same conjunction as the order between parameters is defined in
R_{p}
.

•

If
g
is a parametric guard, let
x
be a stopped stopwatch appearing in
g
.
In both cases (
x
updated to a parameter or a constant) we can test whether
v(g)
is satisfied by testing the conjunction of the constraints
v(C_{\mathsf{stop}})
projected on
x
and
v(g)
projected on
x
as the order between parameters and constants is defined in
R_{p}
.

Therefore,
4.12
and parametric guards
4.13
are still applicable in this context.

With our modified structure of
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM with stopwatches, the core operators for clock updates, time elapsing and comparison with guards are still applicable. We obtain our result by a similar reasoning as
6.4
.

Similarly to
6.5
, we obtain the following result:

Corollary 3
.

Given a bounded S-R-U2P-PTA
\mathcal{A}
and a goal location
\ell
we can effectively compute the set of parameter valuations
v
s.t. there is a run in
v(\mathcal{A})
reaching
\ell
.

8.
Case study

We implemented
EFsynth
for R-U2P-PTAs in
IMITATOR
, a parametric model checker for (extensions of) PTAs
[
AFKS12
]
.

Our class is the first for which synthesis is possible over bounded rational parameters.
We believe our formalism is useful to model several categories of case studies, notably distributed systems with a periodic (global) behavior for which the period is unknown: this can be encoded using a parametric guard while resetting all clocks—possibly to other parameters.

Consider the R-U2P-PTA in
Fig.

1
with six locations, three clocks compared to parameters (
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}x}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}y}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}t}
), one constant (
max
) and six parameters (
{\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0}p}
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0}p_{1}}
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0}p_{2}}
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0}v}
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0}pv_{1}}
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0}pv_{2}}
).

We consider the case of a network of peers exchanging transactions grouped by blocks,
e.g.
a blockchain, using the Proof-of-Work as a mean to validate new blocks to add.
In this simplified example, we consider a set of two peers (represented by
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}x}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}y}
) which have different computation power (represented by
{\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0}p_{1}}
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0}p_{2}}
).
Peers write new transactions on the current block (
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.7,0.3}\mathrm{newTx}}
). If it is full (
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}t}={\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0}p}
), both peers try to add a new block (
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.7,0.3}\mathrm{newBlock}}
) to write the transaction on it. We update
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}x}
to
{\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0}p_{1}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}y}
to
{\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0}p_{2}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}t}
to
0
as the peers have a different computation power, and they start “mining” the block (find a solution to a computation problem).
Either
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}x}
or
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}y}
will eventually offer a solution to the problem (
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.7,0.3}\mathrm{blockSolution_{x}}}
if
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}x}=max
or
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.7,0.3}\mathrm{blockSolution_{y}}}
if
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}y}=max
). If
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}y}
offers a solution,
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}x}
will check whether the solution is correct:
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}x}
is updated to
{\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0}pv_{1}}
to represent its speed to verify an offer.
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}x}
can refuse the offer if the verification is too long (
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.7,0.3}\mathrm{fakeBlock}}
if
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}x}>{\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0}v}
) therefore the mining step restarts.
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}x}
can approve the offer (
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.7,0.3}\mathrm{okBlock}}
if
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}x}\leq{\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0}v}
),
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}y}
is rewarded and the block is added to the blockchain (
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.7,0.3}\mathrm{addBlock}}
).

We are interested in a malicious peer
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}x}
that wants to avoid
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}y}
being rewarded for every new block. Therefore
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}x}
asks:
“what are the possible computation power configurations and verification speed so that
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}y}
can be rewarded”
(
EF(\mathrm{reward_{y}})
-synthesis), considered as a bug state in the automaton.

We run this R-U2P-PTA using
IMITATOR

[
AFKS12
]
4
4

4

Experiments were conducted with
IMITATOR
2.10.4 “Butter Jellyfish” on a 2.4 GHz Intel Core i5 processor with 2 GiB memory. Computation time is less than 1 second.
Sources, binaries, models and results are available at
imitator.fr/static/FORTE19/
.
We set
max=30
units of time and also the upper bound of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0}p}
and
1\geq{\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0}v}>0
unit of time.
IMITATOR
computes a disjunction of constraints so that
\mathrm{reward_{y}}
is unreachable: we keep two relevant ones;

(1)

{\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0}p_{1}}\geq{\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0}p_{2}}
:

{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}x}
has strictly more computation power than
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}y}
in which case
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}x}
always offers a block solution, or has the same computation power than
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}y}
in which case the systems blocks.
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}x}
should invest heavily into hardware to keep its computation power high;

(2)

{\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0}pv_{1}}>{\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0}v}
:
the malicious peer
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}x}
is always faster to verify the solution offered by
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}y}
and refuses it. The blockchain is probably compromised.

Using a parameter valuation respecting one of the previous constraints guarantees that
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}y}
is never rewarded.

Remark 1
.

Even if we have to update all clocks whenever a parameter is met in a guard or in an update, the possibility to update clocks to unknown parameter offers an appreciable freedom in the range of system that can be modeled with R-U2P-PTA. Especially as our parameters can take unbounded rational values in updates and bounded rational values in guards.

However, such a restriction restricts the behavior that can be modeled. Consider the PTA of
Fig.

5
for which the set of possible parameter valuations s.t.
\mathrm{\ell_{3}}
is reachable is

\{v\mid v({\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0}p_{3}})=nv({\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0}p_{1}})+v({\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0}p_{2}})
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}\}
.
This set cannot be computed from a R-U2P-PTA as in the loop transition over
\mathrm{\ell_{2}}
both clocks
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}x}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}y}
must be updated.

\mathrm{\ell_{1}}
\mathrm{\ell_{2}}
\mathrm{\ell_{3}}
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}x}={\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0}p_{1}}
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}x}:=0
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}x}:=0
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}y}:=0
{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}x}={\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0}p_{2}}\wedge{\color[rgb]{0.4,0.4,1}y}={\color[rgb]{1,0.6,0}p_{3}}

Figure 5
.
PTA of
[
AHV93
, Fig. 1]
.

9.
Conclusion and perspectives

Our class of R-U2P-PTAs is one of the few subclasses of PTAs (actually even extended with parametric updates) to enjoy decidability of EF-emptiness.
In addition, R-U2P-PTAs are the first “subclass” of PTAs to allow exact synthesis of bounded
rational
-valued parameters.

In terms of future work, beyond reachability emptiness, we aim at studying unavoidability-emptiness and language preservation emptiness (“given a reference parameter valuation, does there exist another parameter valuation with the same untimed language”
[
ALM20
]
), as well as their synthesis.

We would also study the possibility to stop and start stopwatches without full updates; it would change the
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM structure by creating new clock constraints, but seems promising and the reachability emptiness problem might be decidable as well.

Time bounded reachability is also interesting to study:
given an R-U2P-PTA
\mathcal{A}
, a parameter
\mathsf{max}
and a goal location
\ell
, is there a parameter valuation
v
and a run in
v(\mathcal{A})
reaching
\ell
in less than
v(\mathsf{max})
time?

Finally, we would like to investigate whether our parametric updates can be applied to decidable hybrid extensions of TAs
[
HKPV98
,
BDG+13
]
. For example, we shall find a subclass of hybrid automata that can be reduced to R-U2P-PTA as done with initialized rectangular hybrid automata and TAs in
[
HKPV98
]
.

Appendix A
Proof of
3.4

3.4
(recalled).

Let
d_{1},d_{2},d_{3},d_{4}\in\mathcal{PLT}
. Let
R_{p}
be a parameter region.
If
(d_{1},\triangleleft_{1})\leq(d_{2},\triangleleft_{2})
and
(d_{3},\triangleleft_{3})\leq(d_{4},\triangleleft_{4})
are valid for
R_{p}

then
(d_{1},\triangleleft_{1})+(d_{3},\triangleleft_{3})\leq(d_{2},\triangleleft_{2})+(d_{4},\triangleleft_{4})
is valid for
R_{p}
.

Proof A.1
.

Four cases show up: for all
v\in R_{p}
,

•

v(d_{1})<v(d_{2})
and
v(d_{3})<v(d_{4})
, then clearly
v(d_{1})+v(d_{3})<v(d_{2})+v(d_{4})
and we have our result from
3.3
(
2a
).

•

v(d_{1})<v(d_{2})
and
v(d_{3})\leq v(d_{4})
, then
v(d_{1})+v(d_{3})<v(d_{2})+v(d_{4})
and we have our result from
3.3
(
2a
).

•

v(d_{1})\leq v(d_{2})
and
v(d_{3})<v(d_{4})
, then
v(d_{1})+v(d_{3})<v(d_{2})+v(d_{4})
and we have our result from
3.3
(
2a
).

•

v(d_{1})\leq v(d_{2})
and
v(d_{3})\leq v(d_{4})
, then
v(d_{1})+v(d_{3})\leq v(d_{2})+v(d_{4})
and

(1)

if
\triangleleft_{1}=\triangleleft_{2}
and
\triangleleft_{3}=\triangleleft_{4}
then
\triangleleft_{1}\oplus\triangleleft_{3}=\triangleleft_{2}\oplus\triangleleft_{4}
and we have our result from
3.3
(
2b
).

(2)

if
\triangleleft_{1}=\triangleleft_{2}
and
\triangleleft_{3}={<}
,
\triangleleft_{4}={\leq}
then
\triangleleft_{1}\oplus\triangleleft_{3}={<}
and
\triangleleft_{2}\oplus\triangleleft_{4}
is either
<
or
\leq
and we have our result from
3.3
(
2b
).

(3)

if
\triangleleft_{1}={<}
,
\triangleleft_{2}={\leq}
and
\triangleleft_{3}=\triangleleft_{4}
then
\triangleleft_{1}\oplus\triangleleft_{3}={<}
and
\triangleleft_{2}\oplus\triangleleft_{4}
is either
<
or
\leq
and we have our result from
3.3
(
2b
).

(4)

if
\triangleleft_{1}=\triangleleft_{3}=<
and
\triangleleft_{2}=\triangleleft_{4}=\leq
then
\triangleleft_{1}\oplus\triangleleft_{3}=<
and
\triangleleft_{2}\oplus\triangleleft_{4}=\leq
and we have our result from
3.3
(
2b
).

From
3.3
(
2a
,
2b
) we have that
(d_{1},\triangleleft_{1})+(d_{3},\triangleleft_{3})\leq(d_{2},\triangleleft_{2})+(d_{4},\triangleleft_{4})
is valid for
R_{p}
.

Appendix B
Proof of
3.7

3.7
(recalled).

Let
R_{p}
be a parameter region and
(E,D)
be a
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM for
R_{p}
.
For all clocks
i,j
,
(0,\leq)\leq D_{i,j}+D_{j,i}
is valid for
R_{p}
.

Proof B.1
.

By condition (
4
) in
3.5
and
3.6
(
2
), we have that
D_{i,i}\leq D_{i,j}+D_{j,i}
is valid for
R_{p}
;
the result follows from the fact that
D_{i,i}=(0,\leq)
(again from
3.5
and
3.6
).

Appendix C
Proof of
3.8

3.8
(recalled).

Let
R_{p}
be a parameter region and
(E,D)
be a
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM for
R_{p}
.
For all clocks
i,j
,
D_{i,j}\leq D_{i,j}+D_{j,j}
and
D_{i,j}\leq D_{i,i}+D_{i,j}
are valid for
R_{p}
.

Proof C.1
.

Let
R_{p}
be a parameter region and
(E,D)
be a
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM for
R_{p}
.
Let
D_{i,j}=(d_{i,j},\triangleleft_{ij})
with
d_{i,j}\in\mathcal{PLT}
.
By
3.5
and
3.6
for all clock
i
,
D_{i,i}=(0,\leq)
.
We have
D_{j,i}+D_{i,i}=(d_{j,i}+0,\triangleleft_{ij}\oplus\leq)=D_{j,i}
.
Moreover from
3.3
(
2b
)
D_{i,j}\leq D_{i,j}
is valid for
R_{p}
.
Hence
D_{i,j}\leq D_{i,i}+D_{i,j}
is valid for
R_{p}
.
The same way we prove
D_{i,j}\leq D_{i,j}+D_{j,j}
is valid for
R_{p}
.

Appendix D
Proof of
4.3

4.3
(recalled).

Let
R_{p}
be a parameter region and

(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}(R_{p}).

Let
u_{np}
be a non-parametric update.
Then
\mathit{update}((E,D),u_{np})\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p})
.

Proof D.1
.

The case of a trivial non-parametric update
i. e.,
that updates no clock, is straightforward.

We split this proof in two parts: the first one treats the case of
\mathsf{point}
–
\mathsf{p}
–PDBMs and the second one of
\mathsf{open}
–
\mathsf{p}
–PDBMs.

First we show that applying an
\mathit{update}
on any
\mathsf{point}
–
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM transforms it into an
\mathsf{open}
–
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM.

Claim 1

(
p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\odot}(R_{p})
becomes
p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p})
after
\mathit{update}
)
.

Let
R_{p}

be a parameter region and
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\odot}(R_{p})
.
Let
u_{np}
be a non-parametric update.
Then
\mathit{update}((E,D),u_{np})\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p})
.

Proof D.2
.

Let
R_{p}
be a parameter region and
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\odot}(R_{p})
. Consider
(E^{\prime},D^{\prime})=\mathit{update}((E,D),u_{np})
.
After applying
Algorithm

1
, for all clock
x_{i}
of
(E,D)
where
u_{np}
is defined,

E_{i}^{\prime}=u_{np}(x_{i})
; moreover for all clock
j
,
D_{i,j}^{\prime}=D_{0,j}
and
D_{j,i}^{\prime}=D_{j,0}
.
First note that if
x_{i},x_{j}
have been updated,
D_{i,j}^{\prime}=D_{j,i}^{\prime}=D_{0,j}^{\prime}=D_{j,0}^{\prime}=D_{0,i}^{\prime}=D_{i,0}^{\prime}=(0,\leq)=D_{0,0}
.
For all clocks
i,j,k
, the following inequalities are valid for
R_{p}
:

(1)

(a)

if
x_{i}
is updated:
D_{i,0}^{\prime}=(0,\leq)=D_{0,i}^{\prime}
and therefore trivially it holds that
-1\leq D_{0,i}^{\prime}\leq 0
and
0\leq D_{i,0}^{\prime}\leq 1
are valid for
R_{p}
;

(b)

if
x_{i}
is not updated:
D_{i,0}^{\prime}=D_{i,0}
and therefore
-1\leq D_{0,i}^{\prime}\leq 0
and
0\leq D_{i,0}^{\prime}\leq 1
are valid for
R_{p}
because these constraints were already satisfied in
(E,D)
.

(2)

For all
x_{i},x_{j}
, if neither
x_{i}
nor
x_{j}
is updated,
D_{i,j}
and
D_{j,i}
are not modified so condition
3.5
(
2
) still holds.
If either
x_{i}
is updated, as
D_{i,j}^{\prime}=D_{0,j}
and
D_{j,i}^{\prime}=D_{j,0}
condition
3.5
(
2
) still holds as it holds for
D_{0,j}
and
D_{j,0}
and we apply the same reasoning if
x_{j}
is updated.
If both
x_{i},x_{j}
are updated, condition
3.5
(
2
) trivially holds.

(3)

For all
x_{i}
, if it is updated then
D_{0,i}^{\prime}=D_{i,0}^{\prime}=(0,\leq)
, hence
d_{0,i}=-d_{i,0}=0
and
\triangleleft_{0i}=\triangleleft_{i0}=\leq
; condition
3.5
(
3
) holds.
For all
x_{i},x_{j}
, if neither
x_{i}
nor
x_{j}
is updated,
D_{i,j}^{\prime}=D_{i,j}
and
D_{j,i}^{\prime}=D_{j,i}
so condition
3.5
(
3
) holds as it holds for
D_{i,j}
and
D_{j,i}
.
If either
x_{i}
is updated, as
D_{i,j}^{\prime}=D_{0,j}
and
D_{j,i}^{\prime}=D_{j,0}
, condition
3.5
(
3
) holds as it holds for
D_{0,j}
and
D_{j,0}
.
We treat the case where
x_{j}
is updated similarly.
If both
x_{i},x_{j}
are updated, condition
3.5
(
3
) trivially holds.

(4)

Canonical form is preserved:

(a)

if
x_{i},x_{j},x_{k}
are not updated:
since no clock is updated we have
D_{i,j}^{\prime}=D_{i,j}
,
D_{j,k}^{\prime}=D_{j,k}
and
D_{i,k}^{\prime}=D_{i,k}

since
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\odot}(R_{p})
from
3.6
(
2
), we know that
D_{i,k}\leq D_{i,j}+D_{j,k}
is valid for
R_{p}
;
therefore it remains valid.

(b)

if
x_{k}
is updated and
x_{i},x_{j}
are not updated:
D_{i,j}^{\prime}=D_{i,j}

and
D_{j,k}^{\prime}=D_{j,0}
,
D_{i,k}^{\prime}=D_{i,0}
because
x_{k}
is updated.
Since
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\odot}(R_{p})
from
3.6
(
2
), we know that
D_{i,0}\leq D_{i,j}+D_{j,0}
is valid for
R_{p}
;
therefore,
D_{i,k}^{\prime}\leq D_{i,j}^{\prime}+D_{j,k}^{\prime}
is valid for
R_{p}
.

(c)

if
x_{j}
is updated and
x_{i},x_{k}
are not updated:
then
D_{i,k}^{\prime}=D_{i,k}
because neither
x_{i}
nor
x_{k}
are updated;
since
x_{k}
is updated we have
D_{j,k}^{\prime}=D_{0,k}
and
D_{i,j}^{\prime}=D_{i,0}
;
since
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\odot}(R_{p})
from
3.6
(
2
), we know that
D_{i,k}\leq D_{i,0}+D_{0,k}
is valid for
R_{p}
;
therefore,
D_{i,k}^{\prime}\leq D_{i,j}^{\prime}+D_{j,k}^{\prime}
is valid for
R_{p}
.

(d)

if
x_{j},x_{k}
are updated and
x_{i}
is not updated:
then
D_{i,k}^{\prime}=D_{i,0}
because
x_{k}
is updated;
since
x_{j}
is updated we have
D_{i,j}^{\prime}=D_{i,0}
and
D_{j,k}^{\prime}=D_{0,0}
;
since
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\odot}(R_{p})
from
3.6
(
2
) and
3.8
, we know that
D_{i,0}\leq D_{i,0}+D_{0,0}
is valid for
R_{p}
;
therefore,
D_{i,k}^{\prime}\leq D_{i,j}^{\prime}+D_{j,k}^{\prime}
is valid for
R_{p}
.

(e)

if
x_{i}
is updated and
x_{j},x_{k}
are not updated:
then
D_{i,k}^{\prime}=D_{0,k}
,
D_{i,j}^{\prime}=D_{0,j}
because
x_{i}
is updated;
since
x_{j},x_{k}
are not updated, we have
D_{j,k}^{\prime}=D_{j,k}
;
since
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\odot}(R_{p})
from
3.6
(
2
), we know that
D_{0,k}\leq D_{0,j}+D_{j,k}
is valid for
R_{p}
;
therefore
D_{i,k}^{\prime}\leq D_{i,j}^{\prime}+D_{j,k}^{\prime}
is valid for
R_{p}
.

(f)

if
x_{i},x_{k}
are updated and
x_{j}
is not updated: we have
D_{i,k}^{\prime}=(0,\leq)=D_{0,0}
,
D_{i,j}^{\prime}=D_{0,j}
and
D_{j,k}^{\prime}=D_{j,0}
because
x_{i},x_{k}
are updated.
Since
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\odot}(R_{p})
from
3.6
(
2
), we know that
D_{0,0}\leq D_{0,j}+D_{j,0}
is valid for
R_{p}
;
therefore,
D_{i,k}^{\prime}\leq D_{i,j}^{\prime}+D_{j,k}^{\prime}
is valid for
R_{p}
.

(g)

if
x_{i},x_{j}
are updated and
x_{k}
is not updated: we have
D_{i,k}^{\prime}=D_{0,k}
,
D_{i,j}^{\prime}=(0<\leq)=D_{0,0}
and
D_{j,k}^{\prime}=D_{0,k}
because
x_{i},x_{j}
are updated.
Since
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\odot}(R_{p})
from
3.6
(
2
) and
3.8
, we know that
D_{0,k}\leq D_{0,0}+D_{0,k}
is valid for
R_{p}
;
therefore,
D_{i,k}^{\prime}\leq D_{i,j}^{\prime}+D_{j,k}^{\prime}
is valid for
R_{p}
.

(h)

if
x_{i},x_{j},x_{k}
are updated: we have
D_{i,k}^{\prime}=D_{0,0}
,
D_{i,j}^{\prime}=D_{0,0}
and
D_{j,k}^{\prime}=D_{0,0}
because
x_{i},x_{j},x_{k}
are updated.
Since
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\odot}(R_{p})
from
3.6
(
2
) and
3.8
, we know that
D_{0,0}\leq D_{0,0}+D_{0,0}
is valid for
R_{p}
;
therefore,
D_{i,k}^{\prime}\leq D_{i,j}^{\prime}+D_{j,k}^{\prime}
is valid for
R_{p}
.

(5)

there is at least one clock
x

s.t.

D_{x,0}^{\prime}=D_{0,x}^{\prime}=(0,\leq)
.

Therefore,
(E^{\prime},D^{\prime})\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p})
.

Now we show that applying an
\mathit{update}
on any
\mathsf{open}
–
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM transforms it into an
\mathsf{open}
–
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM respecting
3.5
(
2
).

Claim 2

(stability of
p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p})
under
\mathit{update}
)
.

Let
R_{p}
be a parameter region and
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p})
.
Let
u_{np}
be a non-parametric update.
Then
\mathit{update}((E,D),u_{np})\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p})
.

Proof D.3
.

Most cases are similar to the proof of
1
.

The remaining cases to treat are the cases of
3.5
(
2
).
If
i,j
are different from
0
, and

(1)

if
i,j
are not updated then
D_{i,j}^{\prime}=D_{i,j}
and since it is the case in
(E,D)
, condition
3.5
(
2
) holds.

(2)

if
j
is updated and
i
is not updated then
D_{i,j}^{\prime}=D_{i,0}
and
D_{j,i}^{\prime}=D_{0,i}

and as condition
3.6
(
1
) holds for
D_{i,0}
and
D_{0,i}
in
(E,D)
,
condition
3.5
(
2
) holds in
(E^{\prime},D^{\prime})
.

(3)

if
i
is updated and
j
is not updated then
D_{i,j}^{\prime}=D_{0,j}
and
D_{j,i}^{\prime}=D_{j,0}

and as condition
3.6
(
1
) holds for
D_{j,0}
and
D_{0,j}
in
(E,D)
,
condition
3.5
(
2
) holds in
(E^{\prime},D^{\prime})
.

(4)

if
i,j
are updated then trivially
D_{i,j}^{\prime}=D_{j,i}^{\prime}=(0,\leq)
and condition
3.5
(
2
) holds.

Appendix E
Proof of
4.4

4.4
(recalled).

Let
R_{p}
be a parameter region and
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}(R_{p})
. Let
v\in R_{p}
.
Let
u_{np}
be a non-parametric update.
For all
w
,
{[w]}_{u_{np}}\in\mathit{update}((E,v(D)),u_{np})
iff
w\in(E,v(D))
.

Proof E.1
.

We first treat the case of
\mathsf{open}
–
\mathsf{p}
–PDBMs, the case of
\mathsf{point}
–
\mathsf{p}
–PDBMs will be handled similarly at the end.
We also prove this lemma for a singleton update (only one clock, say
x_{i}
) since updating several clocks can be done by applying several singleton updates in a
0
delay.

\Longrightarrow
for
\mathsf{open}
–
\mathsf{p}
–PDBMs

Let
R_{p}
be a parameter region and
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p})
.
Let
v\in R_{p}
.
Let
u_{np}
be a non-parametric update which updates
x_{i}
to an integer
n
and lets the value of other clocks unchanged.
Consider
(E^{\prime},D^{\prime})=\mathit{update}((E,v(D)),u_{np})

and suppose
w^{\prime}\in(E^{\prime},D^{\prime})
.
We want to construct a valuation

w\in(E,v(D))

s.t.

w^{\prime}=u_{np}(w)
.

Let
w
be a clock valuation
s.t.

for all clock
x_{j}
where
i\neq j
,
w(x_{j})=w^{\prime}(x_{j})
.
That means that for all
j\neq i
,

\mathit{frac}(w(x_{j}))\triangleleft_{j0}v(d_{j,0}),\quad-\mathit{frac}(w(x_{j}))\triangleleft_{0j}v(d_{0,j})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\lfloor w(x_{j})\rfloor=E_{j}

hold from
4.2
since it is the case in
(E^{\prime},D^{\prime})
and these values are left untouched by the update.
Moreover for all
j\neq i
,
k\neq i
,

\mathit{frac}(w(x_{j}))-\mathit{frac}(w(x_{k}))\triangleleft_{jk}v(d_{j,k})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathit{frac}(w(x_{k}))-\mathit{frac}(w(x_{j}))\triangleleft_{kj}v(d_{k,j})

again hold from
4.2
since it is the case in
(E^{\prime},D^{\prime})
and these values are left untouched by the update.

We want a valuation for
w(x_{i})

s.t.

\mathit{frac}(w(x_{i}))\triangleleft_{i0}v(d_{i,0})\quad-\mathit{frac}(w(x_{i}))\triangleleft_{0i}v(d_{0,i})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\lfloor w(x_{i})\rfloor=E_{i}

hold, and for all
j\neq i
,
k\neq i
,

\mathit{frac}(w(x_{i}))-\mathit{frac}(w(x_{j}))\triangleleft_{ij}v(d_{i,j})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathit{frac}(w(x_{k}))-\mathit{frac}(w(x_{i}))\triangleleft_{ki}v(d_{k,i})\quad

(1)

hold.
Let us prove that such a valuation
w
exists.
We set
\lfloor w(x_{i})\rfloor=E_{i}
.

The following lemma proves transitivity of constraints on clocks with respect to constraints in a
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM.

Lemma 1
.

Let
R_{p}
be a parameter region and
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}(R_{p})
. Let
v\in R_{p}
.
Let
w\in(E,v(D))
.
For all clocks
i,j,k
,

\mathit{frac}(w(x_{j}))-\mathit{frac}(w(x_{k}))(\triangleleft_{ji}\oplus\triangleleft_{ik})v(d_{j,i})+v(d_{i,k})
.

Proof E.2
.

Let
R_{p}
be a parameter region and
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}(R_{p})
. Let
v\in R_{p}
.
Let
w\in(E,v(D))
.

Since
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}(R_{p})
, for all
i,j,k
we have from
3.5
(
4
),

D_{j,k}\leq D_{j,i}+D_{i,k}

is valid for
R_{p}
hence since
v\in R_{p}
, we have
v(D_{j,k})\leq v(D_{j,i})+v(D_{i,k})
.
Precisely
that is
(v(d_{j,k}),\triangleleft_{jk})\leq(v(d_{j,i}),\triangleleft_{ji})+(v(d_{i,k}),\triangleleft_{ik})

i. e.,

(v(d_{j,k}),\triangleleft_{jk})\leq(v(d_{j,i})+v(d_{i,k}),\triangleleft_{ji}\oplus\triangleleft_{ik})\text{.}

For all clocks
j,k
satisfying constraints of
(E,D)
,

\mathit{frac}(w(x_{j}))-\mathit{frac}(w(x_{k}))\triangleleft_{jk}v(d_{j,k})\text{.}

Then for all
i,j,k
, either:

•

from
3.3
(
2a
):

v(d_{j,k})<v(d_{j,i})+v(d_{i,k})
and then, regardless of
\triangleleft_{jk}
and
\triangleleft_{ji}\oplus\triangleleft_{ik}

we have
\mathit{frac}(w(x_{j}))-\mathit{frac}(w(x_{k}))(\triangleleft_{ji}\oplus\triangleleft_{ik})v(d_{j,i})+v(d_{i,k})
,
or

•

from
3.3
(
2b
):

–

v(d_{j,k})\leq v(d_{j,i})+v(d_{i,k})
and
\triangleleft_{jk}={<}
,
\triangleleft_{ji}\oplus\triangleleft_{ik}={\leq}
and then
we have

\mathit{frac}(w(x_{j}))-\mathit{frac}(w(x_{k}))(\triangleleft_{ji}\oplus\triangleleft_{ik})v(d_{j,i})+v(d_{i,k}),

or

–

v(d_{j,k})\leq v(d_{j,i})+v(d_{i,k})
and
\triangleleft_{jk}=\triangleleft_{ji}\oplus\triangleleft_{ik}
and then
we have

\mathit{frac}(w(x_{j}))-\mathit{frac}(w(x_{k}))(\triangleleft_{ji}\oplus\triangleleft_{ik})v(d_{j,i})+v(d_{i,k})

which completes the proof.

This completes the proof of
1
.

For all
j\neq i
and
k\neq i
, since
v(D_{j,k})\leq v(D_{j,i})+v(D_{i,k})
from
3.5
(
4
),
we have
\mathit{frac}(w(x_{j}))-\mathit{frac}(w(x_{k}))\triangleleft_{jk}v(d_{j,k})
and

\mathit{frac}(w(x_{j}))-\mathit{frac}(w(x_{k}))(\triangleleft_{ji}\oplus\triangleleft_{ik})v(d_{j,i})+v(d_{i,k})

holds from
1
. Hence

\mathit{frac}(w(x_{j}))-v(d_{j,i})(\triangleleft_{ji}\oplus\triangleleft_{ik})\mathit{frac}(w(x_{k}))+v(d_{i,k})\quad

(2)

holds. Note that
\triangleleft_{ji}\oplus\triangleleft_{ik}
is either
\leq
or
<
.
Note the following trick is inspired by
[
HRSV02
, Proof of Lemma 3.5]
and
[
HRSV02
, Proof of Lemma 3.13]
.
Hence

I=\{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\mid{\mathit{frac}(w(x_{j}))-v(d_{j,i})}\leq{t}\leq{\mathit{frac}(w(x_{k}))+v(d_{i,k})}\mbox{ for all clocks }j,k\}

is a non empty set.
That means that choosing a
\mathit{frac}(w(x_{i}))
with respect to constraints (
1
), recall that they are

\mathit{frac}(w(x_{j}))-\mathit{frac}(w(x_{i}))\triangleleft_{ji}v(d_{j,i})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathit{frac}(w(x_{i}))-\mathit{frac}(w(x_{k}))\triangleleft_{ik}v(d_{i,k})

is equivalent to choose a
\mathit{frac}(w(x_{i}))

s.t.

\mathit{frac}(w(x_{j}))-v(d_{j,i})\triangleleft_{ji}\mathit{frac}(w(x_{i}))\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathit{frac}(w(x_{i}))\triangleleft_{ik}\mathit{frac}(w(x_{k}))+v(d_{i,k})

which is a nonempty set from formula (
2
).
Finally we choose a
\mathit{frac}(w(x_{i}))\in I
, then
w\in(E,v(D))

and it completes the proof.

\Longleftarrow
for
\mathsf{open}
–
\mathsf{p}
–PDBMs

Let
R_{p}
be a parameter region and
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p}){}
.
Let
v\in R_{p}
.
Let
u_{np}
be a non-parametric update which updates
x_{i}
to an integer
n
and lets the value of other clocks unchanged.
Consider
(E^{\prime},D^{\prime})=\mathit{update}((E,v(D)),u_{np})
.
Now suppose
w\in(E,v(D))
and let
w^{\prime}={[w]}_{u_{np}}
.

•

for
x_{i}
, since
u_{np}
is defined,

w^{\prime}(x_{i})=u_{np}(x_{i})=E^{\prime}_{x_{i}}
(
i. e.,

\mathit{frac}(w^{\prime}(x_{i}))=0
) by applying
\mathit{update}
as defined in
4.2
.
By applying
\mathit{update}
as defined in
4.2
,
D_{i,0}^{\prime}=D_{0,i}^{\prime}=(0,\leq)
, hence

-\mathit{frac}(w^{\prime}(x_{i}))\triangleleft_{0i}v(d_{0,i}^{\prime})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathit{frac}(w^{\prime}(x_{i}))\triangleleft_{i0}v(d_{i,0}^{\prime})

hold from
4.2
and
1
.
Moreover we know that for all
j\neq i

-v(D_{i,j}^{\prime})=-v(D_{0,j}^{\prime})\quad\mbox{and}\quad v(D_{j,i}^{\prime})=v(D_{j,0}^{\prime})\quad

(3)

holds from
4.2
, and we also know that

\mathit{frac}(w^{\prime}(x_{j}))-\mathit{frac}(w^{\prime}(x_{i}))=\mathit{frac}(w^{\prime}(x_{j}))\quad

(4)

since
\mathit{frac}(w^{\prime}(x_{i}))=0
.
Hence, combining (
3
) and (
4
), clearly
since

-\mathit{frac}(w^{\prime}(x_{j}))\triangleleft_{0j}v(d_{0,j}^{\prime})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathit{frac}(w^{\prime}(x_{j}))\triangleleft_{j0}v(d_{j,0}^{\prime})

hold in
(E^{\prime},D^{\prime})
,

\mathit{frac}(w^{\prime}(x_{j}))-\mathit{frac}(w^{\prime}(x_{i}))\triangleleft_{ji}v(d_{j,i}^{\prime})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathit{frac}(w^{\prime}(x_{i}))-\mathit{frac}(w^{\prime}(x_{j}))\triangleleft_{ij}v(d_{i,j}^{\prime})

hold.

•

for any two clocks
x_{j},x_{k}
where
u_{np}
is not defined,
w(x_{j})=w^{\prime}(x_{j})
and
w(x_{k})=w^{\prime}(x_{k})
. Hence

-v(D_{0,j}^{\prime})\triangleleft_{0j}\mathit{frac}(w^{\prime}(x_{j}))\triangleleft_{j0}v(D_{j,0}^{\prime})

and

-v(D_{k,j}^{\prime})\triangleleft_{kj}\mathit{frac}(w^{\prime}(x_{j}))-\mathit{frac}(w^{\prime}(x_{k}))\triangleleft_{jk}v(D_{j,k}^{\prime})

hold from
4.2
and
1
since bounds remain unchanged.

Then
w^{\prime}\in\mathit{update}((E,v(D)),u_{np})
.

This concludes the case
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p})
.

Let us now treat the case
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\odot}(R_{p})
.

\Longrightarrow
for
\mathsf{point}
–
\mathsf{p}
–PDBMs

Let
R_{p}
be a parameter region and
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\odot}(R_{p})
.
Let
v\in R_{p}
.
Let
u_{np}
be a non-parametric update which updates
x_{i}
to an integer
n
and lets the value of other clocks unchanged.
Consider
(E^{\prime},D^{\prime})=\mathit{update}((E,v(D)),u_{np})

and suppose
w^{\prime}\in(E^{\prime},D^{\prime})
.
We want to construct a valuation

w\in(E,v(D))\quad\mbox{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s.t.}{}}\quad w^{\prime}=u_{np}(w)

Let
w
be a clock valuation
s.t.

for all clock
x_{j}
where
j\neq i
,
w(x_{j})=w^{\prime}(x_{j})
.
That means for all
j\neq i
,

\mathit{frac}(w(x_{j}))\triangleleft_{j0}v(d_{j,0}),\quad-\mathit{frac}(w(x_{j}))\triangleleft_{0j}v(d_{0,j})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\lfloor w(x_{j})\rfloor=E_{j}

hold from
4.2
since it is the case in
(E^{\prime},D^{\prime})
and bounds remain unchanged
i. e.,

D_{0,j}=D_{0,j}^{\prime}
and
D_{j,0}=D_{j,0}^{\prime}
.
Moreover for all
k\neq i
and
k\neq j
,

\mathit{frac}(w(x_{j}))-\mathit{frac}(w(x_{k}))\triangleleft_{jk}v(d_{j,k})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathit{frac}(w(x_{k}))-\mathit{frac}(w(x_{j}))\triangleleft_{kj}v(d_{k,j})

also hold from
4.2
since it is the case in
(E^{\prime},D^{\prime})
and bounds remain unchanged
i. e.,

D_{k,j}=D_{k,j}^{\prime}
and
D_{j,k}=D_{j,k}^{\prime}
.

Recall that
(E,D)
contains only one clock valuation for each parameter valuation
v\in R_{p}
.

Let
\mathit{frac}(w(x_{i}))=v(d_{i,0})
(or equivalently
\mathit{frac}(w(x_{i}))=-v(d_{0,i})

since by
3.6
we have
(d_{i,0},\triangleleft_{i0})=(-d_{0,i},\triangleleft_{0i})
).
Then, as it is the case in
(E,D)
,

\mathit{frac}(w(x_{i}))\triangleleft_{i0}v(d_{i,0}),\quad-\mathit{frac}(w(x_{i}))\triangleleft_{0i}v(d_{0,i})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\lfloor w(x_{i})\rfloor=E_{i}

hold, and for all
j\neq i
,
k\neq i
,

\mathit{frac}(w(x_{i}))-\mathit{frac}(w(x_{j}))\triangleleft_{ij}v(d_{i,j})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathit{frac}(w(x_{k}))-\mathit{frac}(w(x_{i}))\triangleleft_{ki}v(d_{k,i})

hold, which completes the proof, as
w\in(E,v(D))
and
w^{\prime}=u_{np}(w)
.

\Longleftarrow
for
\mathsf{point}
–
\mathsf{p}
–PDBMs

This case is straightforward and similar to the case
(\Leftarrow)
above of
\mathsf{open}
–
\mathsf{p}
–PDBMs.

Appendix F
Proof of
4.6

4.6
(recalled).

Let
R_{p}
be a parameter region and
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}(R_{p})
.
There is at least one clock
x

s.t.
for all
0\leq i\leq H
,
(0,\leq)\leq D_{x,i}
is valid for
R_{p}
.

Proof F.1
.

Reductio ad absurdum: Let
R_{p}
be a parameter region and
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p})
with at least 2 clocks
i,j
.
Suppose for all clock
x_{i}
there is another clock
x_{j}

s.t.

D_{i,j}<0
is valid for
R_{p}
.
Let
v\in R_{p}
. Then
v(D_{i,j})<0
.

•

Suppose for
x_{j}
,
x_{i}
is the clock
s.t.

D_{j,i}<0
is valid for
R_{p}
.
Then
v(D_{j,i})<0
.
We have
v(D_{i,j})+v(D_{j,i})<0
holds, therefore
0\leq v(D_{i,j})+v(D_{j,i})
does not hold, and hence
0\leq D_{i,j}+D_{j,i}
is not valid for
R_{p}
.
Then
(E,D)
does not respect
3.7
and violates condition (
4
) of
3.5
. So
(E,D)\not\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p})
.

•

Suppose for
x_{j}
, a third clock
x_{k}
is the clock
s.t.

D_{j,k}<0
is valid for
R_{p}
. Then
v(D_{j,k})<0
.
Suppose we have only three clocks. Then for
x_{k}
, either
x_{i}
or
x_{j}
is the clock
s.t.

D_{k,i}<0
is valid for
R_{p}
.

–

Assume this is
x_{i}
.
Then
v(D_{k,i})<0
.
We have
v(D_{k,i})+v(D_{i,j})<0
and
v(D_{k,j})\leq v(D_{k,i})+v(D_{i,j})
by
3.5
(
4
).
Follows that
v(D_{k,j})+v(D_{j,k})<0
and
0\leq D_{k,j}+D_{j,k}
is not valid for
R_{p}
.
Then
(E,D)
does not respect
3.7
and violates condition (
4
) of
3.5
. So
(E,D)\not\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p})
.

–

Assume this is
x_{j}
.
This case is similar (and simpler).

We apply the same reasoning for more than 3 clocks.
Now suppose
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\odot}(R_{p})
. We apply the same reasoning, replacing the argument of condition (
4
) of
3.5

by the fact from
3.6
that
D
is antisymmetric.

Proof of

4.10
(recalled).

be a parameter region.

(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}(R_{p})
.

\mathit{TE}((E,D))\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}(R_{p})
.

Proof G.1
.

We prove our lemma for the two types of
\mathsf{open}
–
\mathsf{p}
–PDBMs and for
\mathsf{point}
–
\mathsf{p}
–PDBMs.

G.0.1.
\rightarrow

3.5
type (
5a
) to (
5b
)

Claim 1

(modification of an
\mathsf{open}
–
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM respecting condition (
5a
) under
\mathit{TE}_{<}
)
.

Let
R_{p}
be a parameter region and
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p})
respecting condition
5a
,
then
\mathit{TE}_{<}((E,D))\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p})
respecting condition
5b
.

Proof G.2
.

Suppose
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p})
respects condition (
5a
) of
3.5
,

i. e.,
we have at least an
x

s.t.

D_{x,0}=D_{0,x}=(0,\leq)
.
Since, in
R_{p}
, we know which parameters have the largest fractional part,
we can determine
\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
from
4.6
.
If more than one clock belong to
\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
then their valuations have the same fractional part.
Indeed, from
4.5
if
x_{i},x_{j}\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
then both
(0,\leq)\leq D_{i,j}

and
(0,\leq)\leq D_{j,i}
are valid for
R_{p}
, and from
3.5
(
2
)
we must have
D_{i,j}=D_{j,i}=(0,\leq)
(
\star
).

Let
v\in R_{p}
.
Assume
x_{i}\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
and
w\in(E,v(D))
,
by letting time elapse,
\mathit{frac}(w(x_{i}))
is the first that might reach
1
.
Moreover, for all
x_{j}\in\mathbb{X}\setminus\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
,
\mathit{frac}(w(x_{j}))
cannot reach
1
before
\mathit{frac}(w(x_{i}))
.
We are going to construct a new
(E^{\prime},D^{\prime})=\mathit{TE}_{<}((E,D))
, which will be an
\mathsf{open}
–
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM respecting condition
5b
of
3.5
.
While detailing the procedure of
\mathit{TE}_{<}
, we are going to prove that
3.5
(
1
) and (
2
) hold for
(E^{\prime},D^{\prime})
.
Further we will prove that (
4
) and (
5b
) also hold.

proof that
3.5
(
1
) holds.

According to the definition of
\mathit{TE}_{<}
(
Algorithm

2
)
the first step is to set a new upper bound

D_{i,0}^{\prime}=(1,<)\quad\mbox{for all }x_{i}\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)

and obviously
(0,\leq)\leq D_{i,0}^{\prime}\leq(1,\leq)
is valid for
R_{p}
.
Then we set new upper bounds for all other clock
x_{j}\in\mathbb{X}\setminus\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
by setting

D_{j,0}^{\prime}=D_{j,i}+(1,<)\text{.}

Indeed,
D_{j,i}
is the constraint on the lower bound of
\mathit{frac}(w(x_{j}))-\mathit{frac}(w(x_{i}))

and since the upper bound of
x_{i}
has increased, this gives the new upper bound of
x_{j}
.
Note that since
x_{i}\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
, from
4.5
and
3.5
(
2
)
we have that
-1\leq D_{j,i}\leq 0
is valid for
R_{p}
for all clock
x_{j}
.
Precisely,
d_{j,i}\in\{0,-p_{1},p_{2}-p_{1},p_{1}-1-p_{2},p_{1}-1\}
for some
p_{1},p_{2}\in\mathbb{P}

where
p_{2}\leq p_{1}
is valid for
R_{p}
.
Hence as
d_{j,i}+1\in\{1,1-p_{1},p_{2}+1-p_{1},p_{1}-p_{2},p_{1}\}
,
we have that
d_{j,0}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{PLT}
,
\triangleleft_{ji^{\prime}}=\triangleleft_{ji}\oplus{<}={<}
so
(0,\leq)\leq D_{j,0}^{\prime}\leq(1,<)
is valid for
R_{p}
.

Note that we cannot have
(d_{j,i},\triangleleft_{ji})=(-1,<)
because even if
(d_{i,j},\triangleleft_{ij})=(1,<)
, since
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p})
we do not have have
0\leq D_{j,i}+D_{i,j}
is valid for
R_{p}
from
3.5
(
4
) and
3.7
.

Secondary we set for all clock
x
regardless of whether they are in
\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)

D_{0,x}^{\prime}=D_{0,x}+(0,<)\text{.}

Since some time elapsed, lower bounds of all clocks are increased.
Moreover, as
(-1,<)\leq D_{0,x}\leq(0,\leq)
is valid for
R_{p}
from
3.5
(
1
),

(-1,\leq)\leq D_{0,x}^{\prime}\leq(0,\leq)
is also valid for
R_{p}
.

Therefore,
3.5
(
1
) holds.

proof that
3.5
(
2
) holds

Third we set for all clocks
x,y
regardless of whether they are in
\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)

D_{x,y}^{\prime}=D_{x,y}

so as
3.5
(
2
) holds in
(E,D)
, it still does.
More intuitively since no fractional part has reached
1
, constraints on differences of clocks and integer parts remain unchanged.

proof that
3.5
(
3
) holds

For all
x_{i}
:

•

if
x_{i}\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
,
D_{i,0}^{\prime}=(1,<)
,
D_{0,i}^{\prime}=D_{0,i}+(0,<)
hence
d_{i,0}^{\prime}\neq d_{0,i}^{\prime}
and
\triangleleft_{i0^{\prime}}=\triangleleft_{0i^{\prime}}={<}
, condition
3.5
(
3
) holds;

•

if
x_{i}\in\mathbb{X}\setminus\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
,
x\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
,
D_{i,0}^{\prime}=D_{i,x}+(1,<)
,
D_{0,i}^{\prime}=D_{0,i}+(0,<)
hence as
(0,\leq)\leq D_{i,0}^{\prime}
is valid for
R_{p}
and
D_{0,i}^{\prime}\leq(0,\leq)
is valid for
R_{p}
,
we have
d_{i,0}^{\prime}\neq d_{0,i}^{\prime}
and
\triangleleft_{i0^{\prime}}=\triangleleft_{0i^{\prime}}={<}
and condition
3.5
(
3
) holds.

For all
x_{i},x_{j}
:

•

if
x_{i},x_{j}\in\mathbb{X}\setminus\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
,
D_{i,j}^{\prime}=D_{i,j}
and
D_{j,i}^{\prime}=D_{j,i}
, condition
3.5
(
3
) holds as it holds for
D_{i,j}
and
D_{j,i}
.

•

if
x_{i}\in\mathbb{X}\setminus\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
,
x_{j}\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
,
D_{i,0}^{\prime}=D_{i,j}+(1,<)
,
D_{0,i}^{\prime}=D_{0,i}+(0,<)
hence as
(0,\leq)\leq D_{i,0}^{\prime}
is valid for
R_{p}
and
D_{0,i}^{\prime}\leq(0,\leq)
is valid for
R_{p}
,
we have
d_{i,0}^{\prime}\neq d_{0,i}^{\prime}
and
\triangleleft_{i0^{\prime}}=\triangleleft_{0i^{\prime}}={<}
, condition
3.5
(
3
) holds. The case
x_{j}\in\mathbb{X}\setminus\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
,
x_{i}\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
is treated similarly.

•

if
x_{i},x_{j}\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
,
D_{i,j}^{\prime}=D_{j,i}^{\prime}=(0,\leq)
, hence
d_{i,j}^{\prime}=-d_{j,i}^{\prime}=0
and
\triangleleft_{ij^{\prime}}=\triangleleft_{ji^{\prime}}=\leq
and condition
3.5
(
3
) holds.

proof that
3.5
(
4
) holds

Now we prove that
3.5
(
4
) holds,
i. e.,
for all clocks
x_{i},x_{j},x_{k}
,
valid conditions such as
D_{i,j}^{\prime}\leq D_{i,k}^{\prime}+D_{k,j}^{\prime}
remain valid in
R_{p}
.
Indeed, when time elapses, all clocks have the same behavior, hence the difference between two clocks does not change without an update.
Precisely, for all clocks
x_{i},x_{j},x_{k}
, are valid for
R_{p}
:

(1)

if
x_{i},x_{j},x_{k}\in\mathbb{X}\setminus\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
: let
x\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
and

•

if
i,j,k
are different from
0
, we have
D_{i,k}^{\prime}=D_{i,k}
,
D_{i,j}^{\prime}=D_{i,j}
and
D_{j,k}^{\prime}=D_{j,k}
;
since
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p})
from
3.5
(
4
), we know that
D_{i,k}\leq D_{i,j}+D_{j,k}
is valid for
R_{p}
;
therefore,
D_{i,k}^{\prime}\leq D_{i,j}^{\prime}+D_{j,k}^{\prime}
is valid for
R_{p}
.

•

if
i,j
are different from
0
,
k=0
, we have
D_{i,0}^{\prime}=D_{i,x}+(1,<)
,
D_{i,j}^{\prime}=D_{i,j}
and
D_{j,0}^{\prime}=D_{j,x}+(1,<)
;
since
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p})
from
3.5
(
4
), we know that
D_{i,x}\leq D_{i,j}+D_{j,x}
is valid for
R_{p}
;
then
D_{i,x}+(1,<)\leq D_{i,j}+D_{j,x}+(1,<)
is valid for
R_{p}
from
3.4
and therefore,
D_{i,0}^{\prime}\leq D_{i,j}^{\prime}+D_{j,0}^{\prime}
is valid for
R_{p}
.

•

if
i,k
are different from
0
,
j=0
, we have
D_{i,k}^{\prime}=D_{i,k}
,
D_{i,0}^{\prime}=D_{i,x}+(1,<)
and
D_{0,k}^{\prime}=D_{0,k}+(0,<)
;
we claim that

D_{i,k}\leq D_{i,x}+(1,<)+D_{0,k}+(0,<)\quad

(5)

is valid for
R_{p}
, which is equivalent to
D_{i,k}^{\prime}\leq D_{i,0}^{\prime}+D_{0,k}^{\prime}
is valid for
R_{p}
.
Since
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p})
from
3.5
(
1
), we know that

D_{x,0}\leq(1,<)\text{;}

(6)

moreover we have

(1,<)+(0,<)=(1+0,<\oplus<)=(1,<)\quad

(7)

Since
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p})
from
3.5
(
4
), we know that
D_{x,k}\leq D_{x,0}+D_{0,k}
is valid for
R_{p}
;
combining with (
6
) and (
7
) we obtain

D_{x,k}\leq(1,<)+D_{0,k}+(0,<).\quad

(8)

Now, since
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p})
from
3.5
(
4
), we know that
D_{i,k}\leq D_{i,x}+D_{x,k}
is valid for
R_{p}

and combining with (
8
) we obtain (
5
) and therefore our result.

•

if
i
is different from
0
,
j=k=0
, we have
D_{i,0}^{\prime}=D_{i,x}+(1,<)
;
from
3.3
(
2b
) we have that

D_{i,x}+(1,<)\leq D_{i,x}+(1,<)

is valid for
R_{p}
.
Hence from

D_{i,0}^{\prime}\leq D_{i,0}^{\prime}+D_{0,0}^{\prime}

is valid for
R_{p}
.

•

if
j,k
are different from
0
,
i=0
, we have

,
D_{0,j}^{\prime}=D_{0,j}+(0,<)
and

(

)

.

.

•

if

is different from
0
,
i=k=0
, we have
D_{0,0}^{\prime}=(0,\leq)
,

and

;

.

if

is different from

,

, we have
D_{0,k}^{\prime}=D_{0,k}+(0,<)

.

.

if

, from

is valid for

.

(2)

if
x_{k}\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)

:

R_{p}
.

(5)

if

G.0.2.
\rightarrow

3.5
type (

) to (

)

Lemma 2
.

Let
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p})
; let
x_{i}\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
,
x_{j}\in\mathbb{X}\setminus\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
.
If
(d_{i,j},\triangleleft_{ij})=(0,\triangleleft)
, then
\triangleleft={<}

Proof G.3
.

Let
x_{i}\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
,
x_{j}\in\mathbb{X}\setminus\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
.
Suppose
(d_{i,j},\triangleleft_{ij})=(0,\leq)
. From
3.5
(
2
) we should have that
(d_{j,i},\triangleleft_{ji})=(0,\leq)

so
3.7
is satisfied, and then
x_{j}\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
.

Claim 3

(modification of an
\mathsf{open}
–
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM respecting condition
5b
under
\mathit{TE}_{=}
)
.

Let
R_{p}
be a parameter region and
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p})
respecting condition
5b
,
then
\mathit{TE}_{=}(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p})
respecting condition
5a
.

Proof G.4
.

Suppose
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p})
respects condition (
5a
) of
3.5

i. e.,
we have at least an
x

s.t.

D_{x,0}=(1,<)
and for all other
j

s.t.

D_{0,j}=(0,\triangleleft_{0j})
,
{\triangleleft_{0j}}={<}
.
First we can determine
\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
. Let
x\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
.
If more than one clock belong to
\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
then their valuations have the same fractional part.
Indeed, from
4.5
if
x_{i},x_{j}\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
then both
(0,\leq)\leq D_{i,j}

and
(0,\leq)\leq D_{j,i}
are valid for
R_{p}
, and from
3.5
(
2
)
we must have
D_{i,j}=D_{j,i}=(0,\leq)
.

Let
v\in R_{p}
. Let
x_{i}\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
and
w\in(E,v(D))
.
By letting time elapse,
\mathit{frac}(w(x))
is the first to actually reach
1
.
Moreover, for all
x_{j}\in\mathbb{X}\setminus\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
,
\mathit{frac}(w(x_{j}))
cannot reach
1
before
\mathit{frac}(w(x_{i}))
.
We are going to construct a new
(E^{\prime},D^{\prime})=\mathit{TE}_{=}((E,D))
which is an
\mathsf{open}
–
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM respecting condition
5b
.
While detailing the procedure of
\mathit{TE}_{=}
, we are going to prove that
3.5
(
1
) and (
2
) hold for
(E^{\prime},D^{\prime})
. Further we will prove that (
4
) and (
5a
) also hold.

proof that
3.5
(
1
) holds

According to the definition of
\mathit{TE}_{=}

Algorithm

3
,
the first step is to fix the value of
\mathit{frac}(x_{i})
to
0
by setting

D_{i,0}^{\prime}=(0,\leq)\quad\mbox{and}\quad D_{0,i}^{\prime}=(0,\leq)\quad\mbox{for all }x_{i}\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)\text{.}

Indeed, when
\mathit{frac}(x_{i})
reaches
1
,
in the constraints expressed by
(E,v(D))
we have to increase the integer part by
1
and set the new constraints on the fractional part to
0
.

Secondary we set new upper and lower bound for all other clock
x_{j}\in\mathbb{X}\setminus\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)

D_{0,j}^{\prime}=D_{i,j}+(-1,\leq)\quad\mbox{and}\quad D_{j,0}^{\prime}=D_{j,i}+(1,\leq)\text{.}

We have to force now upper and lower bounds for other clocks since we know the interval of time that elapsed when
x_{i}
reached
1
.

Note that since
x_{i}\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
,
x_{j}\in\mathbb{X}\setminus\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)

from
4.5
we have that
(0,\leq)\leq D_{i,j}\leq(1,<)
is valid for
R_{p}
for all clock
x_{j}
.
Nonetheless, since
x_{j}\in\mathbb{X}\setminus\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
, we even have
D_{i,j}\neq(0,\leq)
:
suppose
(d_{i,j},\triangleleft_{ij})=(0,\leq)
:
from
3.5
(
2
) we should have that
(d_{j,i},\triangleleft_{ji})=(0,\leq)

so
3.7
is satisfied, and then
x_{j}\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
.
The same reasoning leads to
D_{j,i}\neq(0,\leq)
.

Obviously, we have
D_{i,j}\neq(0,<)
: suppose
D_{i,j}=(0,<)
,
since
x_{i}\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)

then from
4.5

(0,\leq)\leq D_{i,j}
should be valid for
R_{p}
,
which is not from
3.3
(
2b
).

Precisely,
d_{i,j}{\in}\{1,1-p_{1},p_{2}+1-p_{1},p_{1}-p_{2},p_{1}\}
for any two
p_{1},p_{2}\in\mathbb{P}

where
p_{2}\leq p_{1}
is valid for
R_{p}
.
Hence as
-1+d_{i,j}{\in}\{0,-p_{1},p_{2}-p_{1},p_{1}-1-p_{2},p_{1}-1\}
,
we have that
D_{0,j}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{PLT}
and
(-1,<)\leq D_{0,j}^{\prime}\leq(0,\leq)
is valid for
R_{p}
from
2
.

Also note that since
x_{i}\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
, from
4.5
and
3.5
(
2
)
we have that
(-1,<)\leq D_{j,i}\leq(0,\leq)
is valid for
R_{p}
for all clock
x_{j}
.
Precisely,
d_{j,i}\in\{0,-p_{1},p_{2}-p_{1},p_{1}-1-p_{2},p_{1}-1\}
for some
p_{1},p_{2}\in\mathbb{P}

where
p_{2}\leq p_{1}
is valid for
R_{p}
.
Hence as
d_{j,i}+1\in\{1,1-p_{1},p_{2}+1-p_{1},p_{1}-p_{2},p_{1}\}
,
we have that
d_{j,0}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{PLT}
and
(0,\leq)\leq D_{j,0}^{\prime}\leq(1,<)
is valid for
R_{p}
.

Clearly
3.5
(
1
) holds.

proof that

(

) holds

Third we set for all two clocks
i,j
where
x_{i}\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
,

D_{i,j}^{\prime}=D_{0,j}^{\prime}\quad\mbox{and}\quad D_{j,i}^{\prime}=D_{j,0}^{\prime}\text{,}

for all two clocks
x_{j},x_{k}\in\mathbb{X}\setminus\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)

D_{j,k}^{\prime}=D_{j,k}

and for all two clocks

D_{x,y}^{\prime}=D_{y,x}^{\prime}=(0,\leq)\text{.}

Here as we have already proven above that
(-1,<)\leq D_{0,j}^{\prime}\leq(0,\leq)
and
(0,\leq)\leq D_{0,j}^{\prime}\leq(1,<)
are valid for

,

For all
x_{i}
:

For all

, are valid for

:

(1)

if

if

(-1,\leq)\leq(-1,\leq)

if

is different from

,

, we have

and

;
since
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p})
, from
3.7
we know that
(0,\leq)\leq D_{x,j}+D_{j,x}
is valid for
R_{p}
;
moreover, we have that

(1,\leq)+(-1,\leq)=(1+-1,\leq\oplus\leq)=(0,\leq)

and
D_{j,x}+(0,\leq)=D_{j,x}
.
Then we have from
3.4

(0,\leq)\leq D_{x,j}+(-1,\leq)+D_{j,x}+(1,\leq)

is valid for
R_{p}
and therefore
D_{0,0}^{\prime}\leq D_{0,j}^{\prime}+D_{j,0}^{\prime}
is valid for
R_{p}
.

•

if
k
is different from
0
,
i=j=0
, we have
D_{0,k}^{\prime}=D_{x,k}+(-1,\leq)
,
D_{i,j}^{\prime}=D_{0,0}^{\prime}=(0,\leq)
;
we have from
3.3
(
2b
) that

D_{x,k}+(-1,\leq)\leq D_{x,k}+(-1,\leq)

is valid for
R_{p}
.
Hence, as
D_{x,k}+(-1,\leq)+(0,\leq)=D_{x,k}+(-1,\leq)
we have

D_{0,k}^{\prime}\leq D_{0,0}^{\prime}+D_{0,k}^{\prime}

is valid for
R_{p}
.

•

if
i=j=k=0
, we trivially have from
3.5
(
4
) and
3.8

D_{0,0}^{\prime}\leq D_{0,0}^{\prime}+D_{0,0}^{\prime}

is valid for
R_{p}
.

(2)

if

:

if

proof that
3.5
(
5a
) holds

Finally, there is at least one clock
x_{i}\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)

s.t.

D_{0,i}=D_{i,0}=(0,\leq)
.
Hence condition
3.5
(
5a
) holds.

To conclude the proof, we set
E_{i}^{\prime}=E_{i}+1
if
x_{i}\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
and
E_{j}^{\prime}=E_{j}
if
x_{j}\in\mathbb{X}\setminus\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)

We denote by
(E,D^{\prime})
the obtained
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM and
(E^{\prime},D^{\prime})\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p})
.

G.0.3.
\rightarrow

3.6
to
3.5
type (
5a
)

Claim 4

(
p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\odot}(R_{p})
becomes
p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p})
after
\mathit{TE}_{<}
)
.

Let
R_{p}
be a parameter region and
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\odot}(R_{p})
, then
\mathit{TE}_{<}\big((E,D)\big)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p})
respecting condition
5b
.

Proof G.5
.

Suppose
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\odot}(R_{p})
.
Since, in
R_{p}
, we know which parameters have the largest fractional part, we can determine
\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
from
4.6
.
If more than one clock belong to
\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
then their valuations have the same fractional part.

Indeed, from
4.5
if
x_{i},x_{j}\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
then both
(0,\leq)\leq D_{i,j}
and
(0,\leq)\leq D_{j,i}
are valid for
R_{p}
, and from
3.5
(
2
)
we must have
D_{i,j}=D_{j,i}=(0,\leq)
.

Let
v\in R_{p}
. Let
x_{i}\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
and
w\in(E,v(D))
.
By letting time elapse,
\mathit{frac}(w(x_{i}))
is the first that might reach
1
.
Moreover, for all
x_{j}\in\mathbb{X}\setminus\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
,
\mathit{frac}(w(x_{j}))
cannot reach
1
before
\mathit{frac}(w(x_{i}))
.
We are going to construct a new
(E^{\prime},D^{\prime})=\mathit{TE}_{<}((E,D))
which is an
\mathsf{open}
–
\mathsf{p}
–PDBM respecting condition
5b
.
While detailing the procedure of
\mathit{TE}_{<}
, we are going to prove that
3.5
(
1
) and (
2
) hold for
(E^{\prime},D^{\prime})
. Further we will prove that (
4
) and (
5b
) also hold.

proof that
3.5
(
1
) holds

According to the definition of
\mathit{TE}_{<}
(
Algorithm

2
)
the first step is to set a new upper bound

D_{i,0}^{\prime}=(1,<)\quad\mbox{for all }x_{i}\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)

and obviously
(0,\leq)\leq D_{i,0}^{\prime}\leq(1,<)
is valid for
R_{p}
.
Then we set new upper bounds for all other clock
x_{j}\in\mathbb{X}\setminus\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
by setting

D_{j,0}^{\prime}=D_{j,i}+(1,<)\text{.}

Indeed,
D_{j,i}
is the constraint on the lower bound of
w(x_{j})-w(x_{i})

and since the upper bound of
x_{i}
has increased, this gives the new upper bound of
x_{j}
.
Note that since
x_{i}\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
, from
4.5
we have for all clock
x_{j}
that
(-1,<)\leq D_{j,i}\leq(0,\leq)
is valid for
R_{p}
.
Precisely,
d_{j,i}\in\{0,-p_{1},p_{2}-p_{1},p_{1}-1-p_{2},p_{1}-1\}
for some
p_{1},p_{2}\in\mathbb{P}

where
p_{2}\leq p_{1}
is valid for
R_{p}
.
Hence as
d_{j,i}+1\in\{1,1-p_{1},p_{2}+1-p_{1},p_{1}-p_{2},p_{1}\}
,
we have that
d_{j,0}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{PLT}
,
\triangleleft_{j0^{\prime}}=\triangleleft_{j0^{\prime}}\oplus{<}={<}
and
(0,\leq)\leq D_{j,0}^{\prime}\leq(1,<)
is valid for
R_{p}
.
Note that we cannot have
(d_{j,i},\triangleleft_{ji})=(-1,<)
because even if
(d_{i,j},\triangleleft_{ij})=(1,<)
, since
(E,D)\in p\textsf{--}\mathcal{PDBM}_{\blacksquare}(R_{p})
we do not have have
0\leq D_{j,i}+D_{i,j}
is valid for
R_{p}
from
3.5
(
4
) and
3.7
.

Secondary we set for all clock
x
regardless of whether they are in
\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)

D_{0,x}^{\prime}=D_{0,x}+(0,<)\text{.}

Since some time elapsed, lower bounds of all clocks are increased.
Moreover, from
3.6
(
1
) as
(-1,<)\leq D_{0,x}\leq(0,\leq)
is valid for
R_{p}
,

(-1,<)\leq D_{0,x}^{\prime}\leq(0,\leq)
is also valid for
R_{p}
.

proof that
3.5
(
2
) holds

Third we set for all clocks
x,y
regardless of whether they are in
\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)

D_{x,y}^{\prime}=D_{x,y}

since no fractional part has reached
1
, constraints on differences of clocks and integer parts remain unchanged.
As it is the case in
(E,D)
,
3.5
(
2
) holds.

proof that
3.5
(
3
) holds

For all
x_{i}
:

•

if
x_{i}\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
,
D_{i,0}^{\prime}=(1,<)
,
D_{0,i}^{\prime}=D_{0,i}+(0,<)
hence
d_{i,0}^{\prime}\neq d_{0,i}^{\prime}
and
\triangleleft_{i0^{\prime}}\triangleleft_{0i^{\prime}}={<}
, condition
3.5
(
3
) holds;

•

if
x_{i}\in\mathbb{X}\setminus\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
,
x\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
,
D_{i,0}^{\prime}=D_{i,x}+(1,<)
,
D_{0,i}^{\prime}=D_{0,i}+(0,<)
hence as
(0,\leq)\leq D_{i,0}^{\prime}
is valid for
R_{p}
and
D_{0,i}^{\prime}\leq(0,\leq)
is valid for
R_{p}
,
we have
d_{i,0}^{\prime}\neq d_{0,i}^{\prime}
and
\triangleleft_{i0^{\prime}}\triangleleft_{0i^{\prime}}={<}
and condition
3.5
(
3
) holds.

For all
x_{i},x_{j}
:

•

if
x_{i},x_{j}\in\mathbb{X}\setminus\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
,
D_{i,j}^{\prime}=D_{i,j}
and
D_{j,i}^{\prime}=D_{j,i}
, condition
3.5
(
3
) holds as it holds for
D_{i,j}
and
D_{j,i}
.

•

if
x_{i}\in\mathbb{X}\setminus\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
,
x_{j}\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
,
D_{i,0}^{\prime}=D_{i,j}+(1,<)
,
D_{0,i}^{\prime}=D_{0,i}+(0,<)
hence as
(0,\leq)\leq D_{i,0}^{\prime}
is valid for
R_{p}
and
D_{0,i}^{\prime}\leq(0,\leq)
is valid for
R_{p}
,
we have
d_{i,0}^{\prime}\neq d_{0,i}^{\prime}
and
\triangleleft_{i0^{\prime}}\triangleleft_{0i^{\prime}}={<}
, condition
3.5
(
3
) holds. The case
x_{j}\in\mathbb{X}\setminus\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
,
x_{i}\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
is treated similarly.

•

if
x_{i},x_{j}\in\mathsf{LFP}_{R_{p}}(D)
,
D_{i,j}^{\prime}=D_{j,i}^{\prime}=(0,\leq)
, hence
d_{i,j}^{\prime}=-d_{j,i}^{\prime}=0
and
\triangleleft_{ij^{\prime}}\triangleleft_{ji^{\prime}}=\leq
and condition
3.5
(
3
) holds.

proof that
3.5
(
4
) holds

Now we prove that
3.5
(
4
) holds,
i. e.,
for all clocks
x_{i},x_{j},x_{k}
valid conditions such as
D_{i,j}^{\prime}\leq D_{i,k}^{\prime}+D_{k,j}^{\prime}
remain valid in
R_{p}
.
Indeed, when time elapses, all clocks have the same behavior, hence the difference between two clocks does not change without an update.
Precisely, for all clocks
x_{i},x_{j},x_{k}
, are valid for
R_{p}
:

(1)

if

if
i,j,k
are different from
0
, we have

,
D_{i,j}^{\prime}=D_{i,j}
and

from
3.6
(

), we know that

is valid for
R_{p}
;
therefore,
D_{i,k}^{\prime}\leq D_{i,j}^{\prime}+D_{j,k}^{\prime}

,

{\triangleleft}={<}
.
